# Hypotheses of the handing-over-to-readers skill

The claims of this skill that no test backs yet, keyed `H-<id of its line>` and named by that line's record in `rules-provenance.md`, and four claims that no line states because their rule or option is held out of the reference files, keyed `H-held-<name>`. The claim of a clause that left the reference files keeps its line under the id of that clause. Each names its measure and the outcome that would refute it, and in `beside` the published sources found so far, with what they showed and how they bear on the claim in `beside-result` and the conditions they ran under in `beside-bounds`. A hypothesis whose test is planned as our own also names its threshold, its runs and the file that holds the test, and is committed here before the test runs. A hypothesis that a test settles keeps its line, with the result and the new standing.

- **H-R-2** · a question to the addressee that states the fact we know and what his answer changes is answered correctly in its first round more often than the same question asked bare · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of questions the addressee answers correctly in the first round, with no follow-up question from either side, and his time to answer · against: the same question written bare, without the fact we know and what the answer changes · scope: questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share answered correctly in the first round with the pair is not above the share without it, or the shares are equal and the answer takes longer with the pair · beside: external doi:10.29115/SP-2017-0015 Table 3 first-hand; external doi:10.1177/0894439314528091 abstract · beside-result: Jacobson et al. 2017 gave 99 low-income adults, randomized by day, a tailored explanation of why demographic items are asked or none, and the share who would leave an item blank was lower with it on all four items (income 35.2 % against 20 %, proportional difference test p = 0.05, 95 % CI −0.33 to 0.03) and significantly lower on none, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a stated intent to answer survey items, and the purpose of the items in place of the fact and what the answer changes; Zuell, Menold & Körber 2014 found in a randomized web experiment with students that a motivation text raised the frequency of responses to open-ended questions, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: whether an answer is given in place of whether it is correct, and a motivation text in place of the pair; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07, plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/, made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had question-to-operator-with-default E1 (check_question.py reports no problem on the card, its FACT code asking for a statement before the question) pass with the skill and fail without it, the failure on WHY-ASKED alone while FACT passed without the skill too, so the FACT check showed no difference, and client-question-list E1 (check_report.py --profile client reports 0 errors, a check with no code for the fact or for what the answer changes) pass with the skill and fail without it · beside-bounds: 99 low-income, mostly African American adults at two community centres in one US city, three interviewers, four demographic items read aloud with a question on how each would be answered, 2017; a student sample in a randomized web survey with open-ended questions, 2014
- **H-R-5** · when the answer is recoverable from our own material, an agent that researches it first and asks only about the decision that remains sends the addressee fewer questions, at no lower share of tasks done right, than an agent that asks at once · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: questions that reach the addressee per task, and share of tasks completed correctly · against: the same task with the question put to the addressee before the agent researches its own material · scope: questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with research first the addressee receives as many questions per task or more, or a lower share of tasks is completed correctly · beside: external arXiv:2502.13069 §5.2, §5.3, Table 6 first-hand; external arXiv:2603.26233 §4, Appendix C.1 Table 2 first-hand · beside-result: Vijayvargiya et al. compared models as they came on 500 underspecified SWE-bench Verified issues with a GPT-4o user proxy, and the Claude Sonnet models, which explored the codebase first, asked 3.80–4.03 questions against 4.57–6.02 for Deepseek and Qwen 3 Coder, which asked at once, at comparable information gain (0.171 against 0.179 for Sonnet 4 and Qwen) and resolve rates of 41.8% against 46% for Sonnet 4 and Qwen, the source naming no test for these counts, in the claim's direction under other conditions: the strategy was not manipulated and goes with the model; Edwards & Schuster had Claude Sonnet 4.5, on the same 500 issues with a GPT-5.1 user simulator, either ask before any execution or explore and ask whenever a monitoring agent found context missing, and the second resolved 69.40% against 70.40% (permutation test p = 0.621) and asked in 344 tasks against 496, but put 1053 questions against 508, 2.11 against 1.02 per issue over all 500 by division of the counts, the source naming no test for the counts, against the claim under other conditions: the withheld details were held only by the simulated user and were not recoverable from the agent's own material, a monitor that asks during exploration in place of research first and one question after it, only the ask-first baseline was told that the issue was incomplete and not to interact more than three times, and Claude Sonnet 4.5, a model of 2025, ran in OpenHands; a single agent prompted at each turn to check for missing context and ask put 679 questions in 369 tasks and resolved 61.20% against 70.40%, the source naming no test for this difference, against the claim under the same conditions; with Kimi K2.6 the same comparison gave 69.40% against 47.20%, the ask-first baseline failing on instruction following, the source naming no test for this difference, and 3791 questions against 194 · beside-bounds: 500 SWE-bench Verified issues rewritten by GPT-4o to be underspecified, an LLM user proxy holding the full issue (GPT-4o, GPT-5.1), OpenHands agents on Claude Sonnet 3.5, Sonnet 4, Haiku 3.5, Deepseek-v2, Qwen 3 Coder and Llama 3.1 70B (2025) and, in 2026, on Claude Sonnet 4.5, a model of 2025, and Kimi K2.6, the GPT-5.1 proxy answering at most three turns, resolve rates by the issues' tests

- **H-R-24** · a deliverable written by rule 24 — for a client a file in his usual format generated from the source, the source and its internal block kept with us, and for the operator a story closed by the list "Where to look" whose checks carry the exit codes of their output — reaches its addressee with internal content or a check status unlike the check's output less often, and lets the operator verify its claims sooner, than the same deliverable written without the rule · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: the share of deliverables in which the addressee receives an item of the internal block or a check status that differs from the check's own output, and the operator's seconds to a correctly verified claim · against: the same deliverables written without the rule, the source with its internal block delivered as it stands and the checks' status stated in the report's prose · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the rule the share is not lower than without it, or the operator's time to a correctly verified claim is not shorter · beside: external arXiv:2606.18671 §7.1–7.2 first-hand; external doi:10.1186/1471-2288-14-123 abstract · beside-result: HANSEL's interactive evidence pages against the agent's final answer with its source links and step log gave a time to a correctly verified answer of 130.4 against 167.7 s (linear mixed-effects model, t = -4.31, p < .001), in the claim's direction, and a verification accuracy of 82.1% against 75.0% (logistic mixed-effects model, p = .29), not told from chance, under other conditions: answers of web agents checked through interactive pages, not an agent's report with anchors and exit codes; an abstract with a limitations section against the same abstract without it moved readers' confidence in the results by 0.19 (95% CI -0.37 to 0.74, p = 0.50), no difference, under other conditions: a stated limitation of a systematic review read by experts, not a check's status; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07 (plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/), made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had client-question-list E8 (internal.md records closures or default decisions with a basis) pass with the skill and pass without it, and operator-report-with-failing-build E8 (a count of passing tests stands only beside the exit code of the run) pass with the skill and pass without it, so the run showed no difference · beside-bounds: HANSEL: 14 participants of one university, eight tasks on trajectories of o3 and Claude Sonnet 4 web agents, within-subjects, a 2026 preprint; Yavchitz et al.: 300 corresponding authors of trial reports, 30 abstracts of systematic reviews, a randomised trial published in 2014
- **H-Explaining-the-story-is-written-for-the-ear** · a story written for the ear — the text the author would read aloud to a colleague who was away, one claim to a sentence, no clause nested in another, under about twenty words a sentence — is understood and retold more fully by its reader, whether he reads it or hears it voiced, than the same story written for the eye · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: the share of the matter's points the reader retells correctly, or his score on questions about it, after one reading or one hearing · against: the same story written without the rule, as a text for the eye whose sentences join several claims and run past twenty words, and for a client reader the same matter as a question list · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code, read as text or heard voiced · refuter: the share retold or the score with the rule is not higher than without it, for the operator or for a client reader, or it does not fall for sentences past about twenty words · beside: external doi:10.1007/978-3-030-49282-3_18 first-hand; external https://libraryonline.erau.edu/online-full-text/faa-aviation-medicine-reports/AM09-02.pdf first-hand; external https://rosap.ntl.bts.gov/view/dot/8659/dot_8659_DS1.pdf first-hand; external doi:10.3102/00346543211060871 abstract · beside-result: two-sentence news texts of 10-15, 16-20, 21-25, 26-30 and over 30 words a sentence heard through a screen reader differed in workload (NASA-TLX, repeated-measures ANOVA, F(2.26, 45.27) = 19.77, p < .001), lowest at 16-20 words (40.15), no different at 10-15 words (41.22, p = .67) and higher at 21-25 (54.60), 26-30 (64.76) and over 30 words (65.79), each p < .001 against 16-20 in post hoc tests for which the source names no adjustment for multiple comparisons, while comprehension, two multiple-choice questions a text, peaked at 16-20 words (85.7 of 100), which the authors say could be owed to that text's topic, and was lowest at 26-30 (61.9), for which the source names no test, in the claim's direction past twenty words and with no gain below sixteen, under other conditions: sighted listeners blindfolded rather than screen-reader users, lengths spread over texts of different topics, not the same story rewritten, and workload ratings and multiple-choice answers rather than a retelling; in en route air traffic control, message length affected pilots' errors of omission (one-way ANOVA, F(2,45) = 5.97) and readback errors (F(2,45) = 3.61), readback errors rising once a message exceeded two aviation topics, in the claim's direction for one item a spoken message, under other conditions: pilots reading back clearances under workload, items per message rather than words per sentence; the readback error rate rose from .7% to 1.4% as clearances grew from three elements to four, and clearances of five or more elements, 4% of them, held 26% of the errors, for which the source names no test, in the claim's direction for the items a spoken message carries, under the same other conditions; listening against reading the same texts showed no reliable overall difference (g = 0.07, p = .23) and reading ahead when self-paced (g = 0.13, p = .049) and on inferential comprehension (g = 0.36, p = .02), under other conditions: the same text in two modalities, not a text written for the ear against one written for the eye · beside-bounds: Kadayat & Eika: 21 sighted master's and bachelor's students in Oslo, blindfolded, non-native readers of English, the NVDA screen reader, web news texts, 2020; - **H-R-1** · an agent that draws the candidates of its question list only from the places where its work meets the addressee's decision or the data he alone holds, and takes its own decisions itself with a registry line, sends him fewer questions that are its own to decide or that he cannot answer, and misses no more of the questions he must answer, than the same agent listing every open point of its work · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the delivered questions that the addressee marks as the agent's own to decide or as asking for what he does not hold, and share of the questions fixed in advance as his to answer, his decisions and the data he alone holds, that the list misses · against: the same list written without rule 1, its candidates drawn from every open point of the work, the agent's own decisions included · scope: questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of delivered questions marked as the agent's own or as beyond what the addressee holds is not lower with rule 1, or the list with rule 1 misses a larger share of the questions fixed as his · beside: external arXiv:1805.04655v2 §5.2, Table 2 first-hand; external arXiv:2604.14624v1 §5.3, §6.4, Table 3 first-hand · beside-result: Rao & Daumé III ranked the ten candidate clarification questions retrieved for each of 500 StackExchange test posts by the expected utility of their answers (EVPI) against neural and non-neural baselines, scored against the questions two of 10 hired experts marked best, and EVPI reached p@1 27.7 against 25.2 for Neural (p, q, a) and 17.5 for random, Table 2 marking its significant differences at p < 0.05 by a bootstrap test, under other conditions: a model choosing among candidates already retrieved rather than an agent deciding where its candidates come from, and experts' judgements of a question's usefulness in place of the addressee's marks; Vijayvargiya, Viswanathan & Neubig gave an OpenHands agent on Seed OSS 36B, on 500 underspecified SWE-bench Verified issues with a GPT-5 simulated user holding the full issue, the questions of an 8B module trained with rewards for non-redundancy, diversity, answerability and task relevance, and it resolved 36.8% with 3.0 questions on average against 35.6% with 5.1 for GPT-5 and 29.6% with 5.2 for GPT-5 nano (22.4% without clarification, 41.6% with the full issue), the source naming no test for these comparisons, its answerability score alike to GPT-5's (.373 against .369), so on the share of questions the user can answer it showed no difference while asking fewer questions at equal success, and the share of answerable questions fell as the number of questions rose (Figure 3), under other conditions: a trained question module against prompted models rather than one agent with and without a rule on where candidates come from, answerability judged by GPT-5 against the full issue in place of the addressee's marks, and a simulated user; - **H-R-14** · a deliverable written by rule 14 — for a client each item naming the fact itself in his words, with references to document sections, identifiers of runs, registries and files, procedural promises and the account of how we measured kept in our source, and for the operator each claim about code carrying a path:line anchor with the fragment of that line under "Where to look" while the story names the thing by its role — is answered correctly in its first round by the client more often, and lets the operator reach a correct verdict on its claims about code sooner, than the same deliverable written without the rule · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of a client's items answered correctly in the first round with no question back about what a reference points to, and the operator's seconds to a correct verdict on each claim about code beside the share of planted false claims he catches · against: the same deliverable written without the rule, the client's items pointing to document sections, run identifiers and file names in place of the fact, and the operator's claims about code given with no anchor, or with a file name or a path:line anchor alone without the line's fragment · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the rule the client's share answered correctly in the first round is not higher, or the operator's time to a correct verdict is not shorter, or it is shorter only at a lower share of planted false claims caught · beside: external arXiv:2603.11393v1 §4, Table 3 first-hand; external https://www.sfu.ca/~mtaboada/docs/publications/Acarturk_Taboada_Habel_Cross-referencing.pdf §4.2.2 first-hand · beside-result: Irons et al. gave 162 Prolific participants, 54 per group and between subjects, AI-extracted answers on three science-themed news articles, on average 30% of them made incorrect, to judge with the three top-ranked passages a BM25 retriever chose beside each answer, with the whole article as a PDF or with an LLM explanation, and the passages cut the time per judgment from 45.8 to 33.0 s (t(106) = 3.24, p = .003, Holm-Bonferroni corrected, d = .62), in the claim's direction, at an accuracy of 83.1% against 85.7%, and 80.8% with the LLM explanation, that the analysis of variance did not tell apart (F(2,159) = 2.52, p = .08), while with the passages the participants accepted 64.7% of the answers against 71.5% with the article (t(106) = 2.59, p = .011, d = .50), below the 70% that were correct (t(53) = 2.83, p = .01), and with the LLM explanation accepted 86.5% and caught fewer of the incorrect answers than in either other group (F(2,159) = 37.37, p < .001), and in an exploratory split by answer type, which the authors say needs validation, accuracy interacted with the group (F(2,159) = 5.75, p = .004) and on synthesized answers the whole article gave numerically higher accuracy than the passages (t(106) = 2.43, p = .08, corrected), under other conditions: crowd workers checking data extracted from news articles against retrieved passages, not the operator checking an agent's claim about code against the fragment of its anchored line; Acartürk, Taboada & Habel gave 92 students 18 single-screen text-and-figure documents with a bracketed reference to the figure, a descriptive reference or none, 30 to 31 per group, and the posttest score was M = .59 with no reference, M = .59 with the bracketed one and M = .50 with the descriptive one, while the 91 whose eye movements were recorded shifted their gaze between text and figure 4.80 times per screen with no reference, 5.60 with the bracketed one and 4.71 with the descriptive one, with a total gaze time per screen of 52.5, 55.2 and 49.4 s, which the authors read as the descriptive reference costing the least integration effort, and reported a mental effort of 4.46, 4.17 and 4.50 on a nine-point scale, a self-report the authors hold less reliable than eye movements and retention, the source stating that all its results are significant at p < .05 except those explicitly mentioned and naming no test for these scores, no difference in retention against the bracketed reference and in the claim's direction against the descriptive one, under other conditions: references to a figure on the same screen, which the reader holds, not references to sections, runs and files the client cannot open, and retention, gaze and self-reported effort measured in place of answers to a question; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07, plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/, made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had client-question-list E1 (check_report.py --profile client reports 0 errors on questions.md, its PATH, SECTION-REF and IDENTIFIER codes flagging a file path, a section reference and a hash or identifier) pass with the skill and fail without it, on 13 errors of which no line of the output matches the patterns of those three codes, so for this rule the run showed no difference, operator-report-with-failing-build E4 (at least two path:line anchors resolve under the fixture with their fragment on the line) pass with the skill (4 anchors, 0 errors) and fail without it (6 anchors, 3 errors, the three that do not resolve written as a bare file name, Router.java:7 twice and RouterTest.java:25), and question-to-operator-with-default E1 (check_question.py reports no problem on the card, its IDENT code flagging a run, workflow or agent identifier) pass with the skill and fail without it, the failure on WHY-ASKED alone while IDENT passed without the skill too, so the IDENT check showed no difference · beside-bounds: Irons et al.: 162 US-based adults on Prolific, three science-themed news articles with five AI-extracted answers each, passages retrieved by BM25, between subjects, a 2026 preprint; Wunderlich, Reis, Kunde & Memmert had 273 participants of an online study in German forecast the probability that a team would win 70 group matches of the Olympic Games 2024, 4,948 forecasts in all, each match at random with no supplementary estimate or one slightly higher or slightly lower than a realistic estimate labelled as an expert's or an AI's, and over the four labelled conditions, the one with no estimate kept out of the models as a baseline, the higher value raised their forecasts by 4.75% over the lower one (likelihood ratio test, χ² = 103.12, p < .001), the effect 1.32% smaller under the expert's label than under the AI's, an interaction that did not improve the model's fit significantly (likelihood ratio test, χ² = 2.01, p = .156), in the claim's direction under other conditions: a higher number against a lower one rather than against none, a forecast of sport rather than a judgement of one's own effort, and participants for whom the source names no stake in their forecasts; Rogers & Lasky-Fink emailed a request to complete a voluntary survey to 7,002 school district leaders, half in 127 words and half in 49 with five sentences cut from the middle, and 4.8 percent of the short email's readers clicked the survey link against 2.7 percent of the long one's, the source naming no test, in the claim's direction on its second measure under other conditions: the sentences cut were not effort estimates or staffing requests, and a click rather than an answer; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07, plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/, made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had client-question-list E1 (check_report.py --profile client reports 0 errors, its EFFORT code flagging a figure followed by an effort unit) pass with the skill and fail without it with 13 errors, while the EFFORT pattern matches no line of either output, so the EFFORT check showed no difference, the output without the skill asking that one of the client's network engineers spend about a week setting up a second connection, which the pattern does not catch without a digit · beside-bounds: 410 professional software developers of 15 companies in seven countries estimating their own past coding productivity, published 2018; 23 participants, 78% graduate students in computer science and 22% professionals, estimating one specified project in months, second half of 2004; 381 software professionals from Romania, Ukraine, Argentina and Poland estimating the work-hours of three specified projects, published 2016; - **H-R-17** · a report or question for a human reader that states one number per claim, the one the question turns on, and leaves the other figures to the full report has its decisive figure stated correctly by more readers, with no fewer of them making the choice the full figures support, than the same deliverable with the other figures in the same sentences · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who state the decisive figure correctly after one reading, and share who make the choice the full figures support where one choice is best on them · against: the same report or question with the other figures of each claim in the same sentence as the decisive one · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share who state the decisive figure correctly is not higher with one number per claim by a test that tells the difference from chance, or the share making the choice the full figures support is lower · beside: external doi:10.1002/cncr.23959 Results, Table 3 first-hand; Zikmund-Fisher, Fagerlin & Ubel 2010 randomized 838 women of the same panel to two-option pictographs with or without the mortality figures, the arm with them holding the counts of the 2008 two-option pictograph arm (234/364 and 188/378), which the source's introduction calls a comparison with the participants of its original study, and without them more stated the total survival with combined therapy correctly (281/448, 62.7%, against 188/378, 49.7%, χ2 = 14.09, p < 0.001) while the increment the decision turns on showed no difference (355/450, 78.9%, against 294/381, 77.2%, χ2 = 0.36, p = 0.55), and a replication with 714 women fell short on the first gain (64% against 58%, p = 0.13), in the claim's direction on a figure the decision does not turn on, and on the decisive one no difference, the outcome the claim's refuter names, under other conditions: graphics, and the removed figures were the complement of the kept ones; interest in adding chemotherapy fell without the mortality figures (43% against 50%, p = 0.04, adjusted OR 0.68, p = 0.008, and in the replication 21% against 27%, p = 0.06, adjusted OR 0.67, p = 0.03), with no choice defined as correct, which bears on the rule's premise that leaving figures out moves the decision and on the claim's second measure, the choice having moved where the source names none that the full figures support, under other conditions: graphics; Zwijnenberg et al. 2012 randomized 414 members of a Dutch insurants panel and hip or knee patients to tables of 3, 7, 11 or 15 quality aspects of three hospitals, one of them highest or equal on every aspect, and comprehension (mean 4.0, 3.8, 3.9 and 4.1 of 5) and the choice of that hospital (0.97, 0.97, 0.97 and 0.99) differed little and not significantly between the groups by Kruskal-Wallis and chi-square tests, a regression on 349 of them finding 7 aspects understood worse than 3 (β = −0.12, p < 0.05) and 11 or 15 not, so on comprehension no difference in the group comparison and the claim's direction for 7 against 3 aspects alone, and on the choice no fewer with fewer aspects, as the claim's second measure allows, under other conditions: rated tables of hospitals in place of sentences, quality aspects added rather than figures within one claim, comprehension asked of several aspects rather than of one decisive figure, and a choice with no trade-off; Peters et al. 2007 report in their abstract three studies of hospital choice in which formats designed to ease the cognitive burden and highlight the meaning of important information gave higher comprehension and better choices, most for readers lower in numeracy, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: consumers choosing among hospitals from comparative quality information · beside-bounds: women aged 40 to 74 of a US internet panel reading hypothetical breast-cancer vignettes with bar graphs or pictographs, 1,619 in the 2008 study and 838 and 714 in the 2010 one; 414 Dutch insurants-panel members and hip or knee patients reading online tables of three hospitals in November and December 2009; consumers choosing hospitals from comparative performance information, the abstract naming neither sample nor country, 2007

- **H-R-19** · an item of a question list or report that holds one question, standing at its end after its facts, has every decision it asks for answered in the addressee's first reply more often than the same content written with two or more questions in one item or with the question before its facts · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the decisions asked for that the addressee's first reply answers, and share of items that need a second round · against: the same items written with two or more questions in one item, or with the question placed before or among its facts · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of decisions answered in the first reply with one question at the item's end is not above the share with two questions in one item, or not above the share with the question before its facts · beside: external doi:10.2478/jos-2020-0041 §3, Tables 3, 4, 6, 7 first-hand; - **H-R-21** · a procedure the agent proposes to its reader that holds no role, arbiter or step beyond what the contract requires is accepted and carried out as proposed more often than the same proposal with such roles, arbiters or steps added, with no more of what the contract requires left undone · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of proposed procedures the addressee accepts in the first round without asking to change them and then carries out as proposed, beside the share of the checks the contract requires that are left undone · against: the same deliverable proposing a procedure that adds roles, arbiters or steps the contract does not require · scope: procedures an agent proposes in reports and question lists for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share accepted in the first round and carried out as proposed is not higher with the simplest procedure than with the added roles, arbiters or steps, or it is higher only with more of the contract's required checks left undone · beside: external doi:10.1257/aer.20121493 Table 5 first-hand; Munduku & Negre 2026 had 122 participants read explanations of a recommendation whose sentences, fixed in content and wording, were ordered by their own profile of earlier interpretations, another participant's, the population's or a language model's, and they answered 211 of 244 comprehension questions correctly with their own order against 161 of 244 with another participant's (odds ratio 3.82, 95% CI 2.36 to 6.18, Holm p < 0.001), 156 with the population's and 178 with the model's, while acceptance of the recommendation did not differ (chi-square 3.11, p = 0.795), in the claim's direction for the order in which the reader needs the ideas, under other conditions: an order fitted to each reader's earlier interpretations rather than to the dependency of the ideas, and recommendations of cultural activities rather than a report on work · beside-bounds: Bransford & Johnson: high-school students hearing a short passage from a tape, comprehension rated on a scale and ideas recalled, 1972; - **H-Explaining-the-opening-gives-the-whole-matter** · a report or explanation whose first paragraph gives in three to five sentences the task in the reader's words, how it ended, the problem with its cause and what is needed from him is retold and answered correctly after one reading by more of its readers than the same deliverable that opens on its result or gives the matter in fewer than three sentences · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who after one reading retell correctly the task, how it ended, the problem with its cause and what is needed from them, and share who give their decision without asking back · against: the same deliverable with the same parts after its first paragraph, that paragraph opening on the result or giving the matter in fewer than three sentences · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: neither share is higher with the opening of three to five sentences than without it · beside: external doi:10.1016/S0022-5371(74)80003-4 Experiment I first-hand; external https://pdfs.semanticscholar.org/f2eb/ad1fa4020d0647314314457ec2c003516087.pdf Chapter 4, Tables 1-4 first-hand; external ERIC ED171803 Table 1 first-hand; external doi:10.1037/0022-0663.85.2.281 abstract; external doi:10.1016/S0022-5371(72)80006-9 relay · beside-result: Haviland & Clark gave 16 Stanford University students 34 target sentences after a context sentence that named their referent directly and 34 after one that did not, and the students pressed a button to say they understood 181 msec sooner with the direct antecedent (835 against 1016 msec, minF'(1,36) = 6.47, p < .025), in the claim's direction under other conditions: the given information of one sentence placed in the sentence before it rather than the whole matter in a report's first paragraph, and a self-timed judgement of understanding rather than a retelling or a decision; Sternadori had 53 participants each read two news stories told as an inverted pyramid and two told in chronological order, structure varying within readers, and cued recall was .57 against .54 (F(1,50) = 1.32, p = .26), recognition was .82 against .81 (F(1,50) = .01, p = .94), comprehension by sentence verification was .66 against .64 (F(1,49) = 3.20, p = .08) and secondary-task reaction times were 396.4 against 412.7 ms (F(1,44) = 3.92, p = .05), slower for the chronological stories, so that the two differences, at p = .08 and p = .05, both favour the stories that open on their most important facts, bearing on the claim neither way, under other conditions: an inverted pyramid that opens on the most important facts, as the claim's comparison arm opens on the result, compared with a chronological order the claim does not name, rather than an opening that names the task, the cause and the reader's decision compared with one on the result, news stories rather than reports, and memory and sentence verification of story content rather than retelling and decision; Luiten, Ames & Ackerson averaged 135 studies of an advance organizer given before instruction against instruction without one, a mean effect size of .21 on learning over 110 effect sizes and .19 to .38 on retention by interval, the authors stating that they do not test its significance, so the source names no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: introductory material given to learners before a lesson rather than a report's opening, and learning tests rather than retelling or decision; Lorch, Lorch & Inman compared a text with headings, overviews and summaries against the same text without them, and the readers with the signals remembered the topics and their organization better and, in Experiment 2, recalled more topics and less about each, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction on the topics and mixed on detail, under other conditions: three signalling devices together rather than an opening paragraph alone, and recall of an expository text rather than retelling and decision; Bransford & Johnson is a lead not opened, so no result of it is counted · beside-bounds: Haviland & Clark: 16 Stanford University students, 68 context-target sentence pairs shown in a modified tachistoscope, the time to a button press when they felt they understood, 1974; Sternadori: 53 participants of 58 recruited, 88 % of whom were students at a Midwestern university, four news stories each in two structures, every participant reading two in each, read paragraph by paragraph on a monitor, 2008; Luiten, Ames & Ackerson: 135 studies of Ausubel's advance organizer with primary, secondary, college and special-education learners, a meta-analysis presented in 1979; Lorch, Lorch & Inman: readers of one expository text with or without headings, overviews and summaries, recall of its topics and content, 1993
- **H-Explaining-parts-under-headings-cause-before-effect-a-thing-introduced-before-its-use-plain-words** · a report or explanation whose parts each open on a phrase that says what the part is about and stand under a short heading, whose sentences run from what the earlier ones gave to the new thing with the relation between neighbours named by a word, which says what a new thing is before naming or using it and keeps one name for it, and which names things by their role in plain words with no path, identifier or command in the story, is retold correctly after one reading by more of its readers than the same matter written without these practices · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers whose retelling after one reading states correctly the task, what happened and why, and what is needed from them · against: the same report or explanation of the same matter written without the practices: parts with no announcing phrase and no heading, facts listed with no word for their relation and the new before the given, things named or used before they are said, several names for one thing, and identifiers and paths in the story · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of correct retellings with the practices is not above the share without them, for the operator or for a client reader · beside: external doi:10.1016/S0022-5371(74)80003-4 Experiment I first-hand; external doi:10.1080/0163853X.2019.1605257 Table 7 first-hand; external doi:10.1207/s15326950dp2901_3 abstract; external doi:10.5093/ed2013a11 Experiments 1 and 2 first-hand; external doi:10.1016/S0749-596X(03)00028-7 Results, Tables 2 and 3 first-hand; external doi:10.1017/CBO9781139547369.016 Table 13.4 first-hand; external doi:10.1207/s1532690xci1401_1 abstract; external doi:10.1177/0963662519865687 Results first-hand · beside-result: Haviland & Clark gave 16 Stanford students pairs of sentences whose second sentence had its given information directly in the first or only by inference, and the second was understood in 835 against 1016 msec, minF'(1,36) = 6.47, p < .025, in the claim's direction for the given before the new, under other conditions: the time to understand one sentence of a pair rather than the retelling of a report; Kleijn, Pander Maat & Sanders added or removed causal, temporal, contrastive and additive connectives in 20 authentic Dutch texts filled in as cloze tests by 794 adolescents, and in a generalized linear mixed-effect model removing the connective lowered correct answers in the sentences of contrastive relations (−0.060 + −0.147 = −0.207, interaction p = .006), marginally in those of causal relations (−0.060, z = −1.935, p = .053), not in those of temporal relations (interaction p = .870) and raised them in those of additive relations (−0.060 + 0.165 = 0.105, interaction p = .004), the effect lying in the sentences that hold the connective and not over the whole text, save in a post-hoc analysis of the ten more difficult texts (β = −0.067, SE = 0.022, p = .002), bounding the claim by the kind of relation: in its direction for a word of contrast and marginally for a word of cause, with no effect for a word of time and against it for an additive word, under other conditions: adolescents' cloze scores in the sentences that hold the connective, on Dutch school and public-information texts, rather than a retelling of the whole matter; Sanders & Noordman compared expository texts whose relations were marked by signalling phrases with the same texts unmarked, and marking gave faster processing and no difference in recall, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction on reading time and not on recall, under other conditions: readers of expository texts in a laboratory; Lorch, Lemarié & Chen had 120 college students outline a printed text with headings, with preview sentences or with neither, and outlines held more topics with headings (Tukey HSD t(108) = 5.20, d = 1.16) and with previews (t(108) = 3.40, d = 0.76) than with neither, and 134 students reading for understanding recalled more subtopics with headings than with previews (4.33 against 3.15, F(1, 130) = 10.07), in the claim's direction for parts announced and headed, under other conditions: students recalling or outlining a textbook-like text rather than retelling a report; Metzing & Brennan had 24 undergraduates settle with a speaker on a name for an object, and they touched it 540 ms later when that speaker switched to a new name than when a new speaker used it (planned contrast t(23) = 2.30, p = .031), and in 1897 against 2595 ms when the original speaker kept or switched the name, the main effect of the expression reliable by subjects and not by items, in the claim's direction for one name per thing, under other conditions: spoken reference to objects in a matching task rather than a written text; Mayer & Pilegard list 16 experiments in which learners received pre-training in the names and characteristics of a system's components before a multimedia lesson or no pre-training, with effect sizes on problem-solving transfer whose median is 0.75 and 13 of which favour pre-training, the chapter naming no test for the tally, while learners with low prior knowledge showed 1.22, 1.15 and 1.84 and learners with high prior knowledge 0.11, −0.68 and −0.38, effect sizes with no test or interval, in the claim's direction for a thing introduced before its use to readers who lack the matter and bounded by prior knowledge for readers who already hold the things, under other conditions: students and apprentices given pre-training in a system's components as a separate phase before a multimedia lesson rather than a thing introduced before its use in running prose; McNamara, Kintsch, Songer & Kintsch varied the local and global coherence of a science text, and students who knew little of the domain benefited from the coherent text while high-knowledge readers benefited from a minimally coherent one, the abstract placing the gains of active processing chiefly on the inference, problem-solving and sorting tasks of situational understanding rather than on free recall and text-based questions, giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction for readers who know little of the domain and bounded by reader knowledge for those who know it, under other conditions: school students learning from a science text, measured on inference, problem solving and sorting rather than on a retelling; Bullock, Colón Amill, Shulman & Dixon randomly assigned 650 adults of a US online panel to science paragraphs with or without jargon, and processing fluency was lower with jargon (M = 4.57 against 5.27, F(1, 636) = 76.03, p < .001), in the claim's direction for plain words, under other conditions: the fluency lay readers report rather than a retelling, and the jargon of emerging technologies rather than identifiers of code · beside-bounds: 16 Stanford University students reading sentence pairs in a tachistoscope, 1974; 794 Dutch secondary-school students aged 13–16, cloze tests on 20 educational and public-information texts, 2019; readers of expository texts with problem-solution and list relations, 2000; 134 and 120 college students of introductory psychology reading a printed expository text, 2013; 24 undergraduates of one US university in a spoken matching task with a confederate, 2003; students and apprentices in 16 experiments of multimedia lessons on brakes, pumps, electrical systems, geology, spreadsheets and other mechanisms, collected 2014; junior high school and other students reading a science text, 1996; 650 US panel adults reading science paragraphs, 2019
- **H-Explaining-parts-under-headings-cause-before-effect-a-thing-introduced-before-its-use-plain-words-causal-connective** · a word that names the causal relation between two neighbouring sentences, such as "because" or "so", raises the reader's comprehension of that relation over the same two sentences with the relation left to him · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of correct answers to a question on the relation between two neighbouring facts of the text · against: the same neighbouring sentences with no connective between them · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of correct answers with the causal connective is not above the share without it · beside: external doi:10.1080/0163853X.2019.1605257 Table 7 first-hand; external doi:10.1207/s15326950dp2901_3 abstract; external doi:10.1080/01690960444000151 abstract · beside-result: Kleijn, Pander Maat & Sanders removed or added the connective of 100 causal relations among 193 manipulated in 20 authentic Dutch texts read as cloze tests by 794 adolescents, and removing it lowered correct answers in the host sentences by an estimate of −0.060 (odds ratio 0.942, z = −1.935, p = .053) in a generalized linear mixed-effect model, in the claim's direction short of significance, while the same removal for contrastive relations lowered them significantly more, under other conditions: adolescents' cloze scores on school and public-information texts; Sanders & Noordman found that signalling phrases marking problem-solution and list relations sped processing and did not affect recall, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction on reading time and not on recall, under other conditions: a laboratory reading of expository texts; Maury & Teisserenc joined the clauses of popular science texts by "because" or separated them by a full stop, and the connective did not enhance the causal inferences readers made and gave faster end-of-clause reading times, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction on reading time and not on inference, under other conditions: short texts read in a laboratory · beside-bounds: 794 Dutch secondary-school students aged 13–16, cloze tests on 20 educational and public-information texts, 2019; readers of expository texts with problem-solution and list relations, 2000; readers of short popular science texts in two experiments, 2005
- **H-Explaining-parts-under-headings-cause-before-effect-a-thing-introduced-before-its-use-plain-words-parts-named-first** · giving the names and characteristics of a mechanism's parts before the account of how they work together raises the reader's understanding of the account over the same account with the parts introduced only inside it · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of correct answers to questions that apply the mechanism to a case the text does not state · against: the same causal account with no part named and characterised before it · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of correct answers with the parts named first is not above the share without, for readers new to the parts · beside: external doi:10.1017/CBO9781139547369.016 Table 13.4 first-hand · beside-result: Mayer & Pilegard list 16 experiments that gave learners pre-training in the names and characteristics of a system's components before a multimedia lesson or no pre-training, and the principle "was supported in 13 out of 16 experimental tests, yielding a median effect size of 0.75" on problem-solving transfer, the chapter naming no test for the tally and no sample sizes, in the claim's direction for learners with low prior knowledge (1.22, 1.15 and 1.84 among them) and not for those with high prior knowledge (0.11, −0.68 and −0.38), under other conditions: students and apprentices learning a mechanism from a narrated animation, a simulation or an instructional text rather than a reader of a report · beside-bounds: students and apprentices in 16 experiments of multimedia lessons on brakes, a tire pump, electrical appliances and circuits, geology, spreadsheet mathematics, statistics, neural networks and pulley systems, tallied in a handbook chapter of 2014
- **H-Explaining-where-to-look** · a report whose story is closed by a list of where to look — at most five lines, each naming in plain words what is there and then its path:line anchor with the fragment of the line that backs a claim, the checks that ran with their counts and exit codes taken from their output, in the order of the story — lets its reader verify more of its claims correctly and sooner, and retell the story no less fully, than the same report with that evidence written into the story's sentences or gathered in one dense line · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: the share of the report's claims the reader verifies correctly against the files and the check output, his seconds to a correctly verified claim, and the share of the matter's points he retells correctly after one reading · against: the same report with its anchors, fragments and exit codes written into the sentences of the story or gathered in one dense line of evidence, and for a client reader, who opens nothing of ours, the same matter as a question list without the list · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the list the share of claims verified correctly is not higher and the time to a correctly verified claim is not shorter, or the share of points retold is lower; or a client reader answers and retells no better with the list than with the question list · beside: external arXiv:2603.11393v1 §3.2.3, §4, Table 3 first-hand; external arXiv:2606.18671v2 §6, §7.1–7.2 first-hand; arXiv:2606.18671v2: evidence pages extracted from a web agent's run with the evidence highlighted against the agent's final response with its visited source links, both with the step log, gave a time to a correctly verified answer of 130.4 against 167.7 s (linear mixed-effects model, t = -4.31, p < .001), in the claim's direction, and an accuracy of 82.1% against 75.0% (p = .29), not told from chance, under other conditions: interactive pages behind a web agent's answer, not a list closing a report; arXiv:2601.14611v1: every source card in a list after each chatbot answer against inline badges with a collapsible list, hover cards and a sidebar aligned with the citations gave the longest average reading session, 11.65 s against 7.00 s for hover cards (Dunn's test p = .007) and not different from the collapsible list (p = .25) or the sidebar (p = .16), and no coefficient of the list condition reached p < .05 on any critical-thinking score, no difference on the measure closest to the claim, under other conditions: lay participants writing essays from a research assistant's cited answers, a list of sources beside inline citation badges, not anchors with fragments; arXiv:2510.00361v3: evidence excerpts with their counts and explanations beside each claim of an AI answer against a chat-with-papers interface gave revised claims an overall grade of 4.00 against 2.87 (Wilcoxon W = 3.0, p = 0.004, five of six criteria significant after Holm-Bonferroni, adjusted p ≤ 0.024), in the claim's direction for the fragment beside the claim, under other conditions: graduate students reviewing a scientific claim in place, not a list after a story; doi:10.1177/0098628320901384: an expository text with in-text APA citations against the same text without them, less skilled structure builders scored significantly lower on a comprehension quiz and read faster with the citations, and skilled ones scored equally and read slower, the abstract giving no figures, in the claim's direction for keeping the evidence out of the prose, under other conditions: readers of an expository text in a study of textbook comprehension, author-date citations rather than file anchors; - **H-Explaining-the-reader-s-decision-closes-the-story-its-options-in-a-picture** · a report or explanation for a human reader that closes with his decision, each option shown as a block of what it gives and what it costs, the author's choice with its reason after the options and one question last, leads the reader to choose the option that fits his own goal, with no question back, more often than the same report with the options told in one paragraph and known by their number · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who after one reading choose the option that fits the goal they stated before reading and send no question back about what an option means, beside the share of the options' gains and costs they restate correctly · against: the same report with the same options told in one paragraph of running text, known by their number, with no gain and cost stated for each, and the author's choice and the question placed where the author happens to put them · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of readers who choose the option that fits their goal with no question back is not higher with the closing decision and its picture than with the paragraph, whatever the share of gains and costs they restate · beside: external doi:10.1098/rsos.190876 §3 Results, Table 1 first-hand; WNE_WP273: 115 laboratory participants, 57 given the options as text and 58 as a table, choosing among tokens of known money value in nine tasks, made the payoff-maximising choice in 87.3 % against 86.6 % of choices (random-effects logit, text coefficient 0.028, standard error 0.371, not significant), no difference, in the first task alone more often from the table (logit text coefficient −0.852 at the 10 % level, −1.322 at the 5 % level with covariates, 113 observations), in the claim's direction, and took 48.2 against 36.5 seconds a choice, longer with text by a random-effects linear model, while those who never or only sometimes calculated the tokens' values chose in 19 seconds with the table against 49 with text and made the payoff-maximising choice 64 % against 78 % of the time, against the claim, a subgroup of limited number for which the source names no test, under other conditions: priced tokens in an economics experiment rather than the options of a report, a table against text rather than options as blocks of gains and costs, the author's choice after them and one question last against a paragraph, in a working paper; - **H-Explaining-a-question-without-a-one-sentence-answer-marks-the-author-s-gap** · an agent that, before it writes a deliverable, tries to write the answer to each of the reader's questions in one plain sentence with its evidence and closes every question it cannot so answer by reading the code, running the check or computing the number delivers fewer answers that are wrong or stand without evidence than the same agent writing from the same material without that step · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the reader's questions whose answer in the delivered text is wrong against the work's record (the code, a run's output, a computed number) or is stated as settled with no evidence, beside the share of questions the text answers or names as open · against: the same deliverable written by the same agent from the same material for the same reader's questions with no one-sentence answers written and no gaps closed before the text · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of wrong or unsupported answers with the step is not lower than without it, or it is lower only because the text answers fewer of the questions without naming the rest as open · beside: external doi:10.1207/s15516709cog2605_1 §2.1.2, §2.2, §2.5.3, §3.2 first-hand; 12 independent raters (α = .946) judged those explanations below their authors' first ratings (p < .001) and no differently from their ratings after a diagnostic question, and 37 participants who wrote out procedures showed a slight non-significant rise (TIME × DOMAIN F(1, 132) = 40.934), in the claim's direction on its premise that an attempted explanation shows its author a gap he did not see, under other conditions: people rating their own understanding of everyday devices against their own earlier rating rather than a deliverable's answers checked against a record, and a free-length causal explanation in place of a one-sentence answer; Dhuliawala et al. 2023 had Llama 65B plan verification questions on its own draft, answer them apart from it and revise, against the few-shot draft, and precision on 56 Wikidata list questions rose from 0.17 to 0.36 and FactScore on biographies from 55.9 to 71.4, the source names no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: the questions check a draft already written and are answered from the model's own knowledge rather than by reading, running or computing, and no human reader; Şenol 2026 had eleven models name the information a question needs and its support before committing, or abstain, against chain-of-thought, and on 817 TruthfulQA multiple-choice questions the share of wrong commitments fell from 13.1% to 8.9% and answered accuracy rose from 86.9% to 89.7% with 87.6% of questions answered, lower for every model (exact one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank p = 0.00049), in the claim's direction under other conditions: the gap is closed by abstaining rather than by research, and the questions are multiple-choice factual items rather than a reader's questions about a work · beside-bounds: doi:10.1207/s15516709cog2605_1: 16 Yale graduate students, 33 and 12 Yale undergraduates and 37 participants on procedures, self-ratings on a 7-point scale of understanding everyday devices before and after writing explanations, 2002; - **H-Explaining-why-this-way-answered-with-what-happens-without-it** · an explanation that answers why a thing was done this way with the input or state under which the alternative fails, with its number, leaves more of its readers able to say correctly why the alternative was not taken, and fewer of them asking back about it, than the same explanation that gives the reasons for the chosen way alone · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who after one reading state correctly the input or state under which the rejected alternative fails, beside the share who state the chosen way's own reason correctly, and share of readers who ask back why the alternative was not taken · against: the same deliverable answering why this way with the reasons for the chosen way alone, the alternative's failing input or state and its number left out · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share stating the alternative's failure correctly is not higher with the clause and the share asking back is not lower, or readers with the clause state the chosen way's own reason correctly less often than readers without it · beside: external arXiv:2410.04253v2 §7.1.1, Figure 4 first-hand; external doi:10.1145/1518701.1519023 Experiment 2 first-hand; external arXiv:2409.13427v1 §III-A, Table I first-hand; external arXiv:2502.12354v2 §5.1, Figure 4 first-hand; external the review of the explanation page by four of its readers (research/2026-11-22_explainer-review-evidence.md § The review, the record kept by the repository owner) relay · beside-result: arXiv:2410.04253v2: 628 US crowdworkers recruited on Prolific in five arms, each making 14 exercise choices for fictitious characters with a simulated AI's suggestion, an explanation of why the AI's choice beats the one presented as what many people would choose, naming only the concepts in which the two choices differ, against an explanation justifying the AI's choice alone with all the features that contribute to it, unaided accuracy on 5 later choices controlled for the pre-test 0.47 against 0.39 (ANCOVA, F(1,260) = 40.99, p = 0.02 after Holm-Bonferroni correction, d = 0.35 [0.11, 0.60]) at accuracy during the task of 0.56 against 0.58 (n.s.), a random foil reaching 0.41, below the predicted foil at p = 0.09 (d = 0.26 [0.02, 0.50]) and with no test against the unilateral explanation named, and the participant's own choice used as the foil after deciding 0.43 (against the unilateral explanation p = n.s., d = 0.16 [−0.08, 0.40]), in the claim's direction for a foil the reader is likely to hold and for neither other foil, under other conditions: later independent choices in a lay task rather than a reader's account of a design choice, with asking back not measured, the differing concepts given in place of the full justification rather than beside it, and the foil's weaker points named in words rather than the input under which it fails with its number; doi:10.1145/1518701.1519023 Experiment 2: 158 Mechanical Turk workers in five arms shown the outputs of a decision-tree classifier with inputs relabelled A, B and C, 31 given an explanation of why it did not give the other output in place of one of why it gave its own, which 30 were given, fewer fully correct reasons for test outputs (contrast of Why with Why Not F[1,153]=23.2, p<.001), 0.79 against 1.19 correct rules (F[1,59]=6.16, p<.02) and 0.23 against 0.11 extraneous ones (F[1,59]=8.276, p<.006), and 74.2% against 80.0% correct answers, the source setting both apart from 61.7% without explanation (F[1,152]=51.6, p<.001) and not from each other, the readers of why not "not statistically better at providing Why Not reasons than Why reasons", the source naming no test for this, against the claim, under other conditions: the alternative's failing conditions given in place of the reason for the output rather than beside it, a classifier's output rather than a design choice, and asking back not measured; arXiv:2409.13427v1: 128 Prolific participants reviewing a week's schedule recommended for two smart-home users, a group that asked at least five questions of why one schedule and not another and saw the alternative's bill increase in pence against a group that saw the recommended schedule alone, ease of understanding on a 7-point scale 5.578 against 5.215 (p = .055) and 5.328 against 5.077 (p = .169) and satisfaction higher at p = .033 and p = .01, one-tailed Welch t-tests at α = .05, in the claim's direction on understanding without reaching that level, under other conditions: against no explanation at all rather than the reasons for the schedule alone, self-rated rather than tested understanding, and questions the participant chose; arXiv:2502.12354v2: 698 of 839 US Prolific participants each ranking six written explanations of one AI decision in one of six scenarios, a complete explanation naming all causes against contrastive and counterfactual explanations naming only the causes that differ from another case, the complete explanation rated higher on all explanatory values overall, understandability among them, and the counterfactual one second-to-worst on sufficiency and understandability, while by scenario a contrastive explanation against the person's own earlier state was rated comparable to the complete one in the driving-app scenario and one against others with the opposite outcome in the undesirable medical scenario, and in the loan scenario the counterfactual and that contrastive explanation were the most popular choices and the complete one was favoured less, the methods naming Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Bonferroni correction for pairs of styles and §5.1 naming no test for these rankings, against the claim, the loan scenario in its direction and the driving-app and undesirable medical ones level, under other conditions: rated preference rather than a tested account, and the contrast confounded with giving fewer causes; the review of the explanation page: its four readers did not take the removal of a part from the finished algorithm as proof that the part is needed, and asked for one case in which a simpler scheme of their naming fails, as the owner's summary of the meeting says, a neighbouring question: which alternative the answer is made against, with no comparison of readers' answers, and the record of the meeting was not opened in this repository · beside-bounds: arXiv:2410.04253v2: US adult crowdworkers on Prolific, an exercise-recommendation task with a simulated AI and explanations written by GPT-4 from an expert model, CHI 2025; - **H-Explaining-what-was-not-done-or-not-verified-only-where-it-changes-what-the-reader-does-or-trusts** · a report that states in a first-person sentence each thing not done or not verified that would change what its reader does or trusts, and no other, leads him to act correctly on the unverified points more often than the same report without those sentences, and no less often than the same report that also states the gaps that change nothing · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the unverified points on which the reader acts correctly before relying on the work (checks it, holds back or asks), beside the share of verified claims he wrongly doubts and his reading time · against: the same report without the sentences on what was not done or not verified; and the same report that also states every gap that changes nothing for its reader · scope: reports and explanations an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of unverified points acted on correctly with the sentences is not above the share without them; or the report that also states the gaps that change nothing gives an equal or higher share with no more verified claims wrongly doubted and no longer reading time · beside: external doi:10.1186/1471-2288-14-123 Results and Table 4 first-hand; external arXiv:2405.00623v2 §4 Table 1 and §5.1 first-hand; external arXiv:2605.28571v1 §5.2 and §5.4 first-hand; external doi:10.1037/xap0000232 abstract; external arXiv:2610.20003v2 §5.2 and Appendix H Table 37 first-hand; external arXiv:2611.08232v1 §4.2 first-hand · beside-result: Yavchitz et al. 2014 randomized 300 clinicians to one of 30 abstracts of systematic reviews with or without a limitations section of at most two sentences, and their confidence in the results on a scale from 0 to 10 was 4.4 with it against 4.6 without (linear mixed model, mean difference 0.2, 95% CI −0.4 to 0.7, p = 0.5), no secondary outcome differing, against the claim that a stated limitation changes what the reader trusts, under other conditions: expert readers, the limitations of published evidence rather than of the author's own work, and a confidence rating rather than an action; Kim et al. 2024 gave 404 crowd workers eight medical yes or no questions and a fictional LLM search engine right on four of them, and those who saw half of its answers open with a first-person expression of uncertainty answered correctly more often than those who saw none (72.8% against 63.9%, mixed-effects model, p < 0.01) and agreed with it less (74.8% against 80.9%, p < 0.05), in the claim's direction under other conditions: a hedge on an answer in place of a statement of what was not checked, lay readers and medical questions; in its exploratory analysis the expression lowered accuracy on answers that were right (92.2% to 89.2%) and raised it on answers that were wrong (43.6% to 52.0%), given as model estimates with standard errors, so the source names no test for these figures, in the direction of the clause that leaves out a gap that changes nothing; Villavicencio, Pan & Wang 2026 gave 192 Prolific participants, 48 per arm, gpt-4o-mini answers to challenging medical questions with uncertainty shown per word, per reasoning step, per response or not at all, and task accuracy did not differ (0.657 without against 0.648, 0.646 and 0.617, mixed-effects models with Wald tests, no significant difference) while uncertainty per reasoning step lowered link clicking (0.318 against 0.557, p < .05, OR = 0.42) and web searches (0.247 against 0.342, p < .05, OR = 0.81), and under it participants clicked links less when the answer was right than when it was wrong (0.118 against 0.204, p < .05, OR = 0.49) and searched less (0.157 against 0.339, p < .05, OR = 0.43), which the baseline did not show, the interaction of answer correctness with condition reported significant for link usage (p < .05), a finding the claim does not cover, under other conditions: model uncertainty shown as a score or highlight in place of a sentence on what was not done or verified, lay readers and medical questions; Bott et al. 2019 found in three experiments that news reports with a caveat such as "Further research is needed to validate the results" were judged more cautious than without it, with interest and comprehensibility unaffected, and in a fourth that caveats did not change journalism students' judgements of newsworthiness, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction for what the reader trusts under other conditions: readers of science news and a judged caution rather than an action; Xu et al. 2026 had a model supervisor write its next instruction from coding agents' reports on 37 tasks, and adding a task-state fact the reports had omitted changed the instruction in 0.718 of units against 0.525 when a fact already available was added (paired difference +18.9 points, task-clustered 95% CI +10.2 to +28.0), in the direction of the condition that a stated gap counts where the reader lacks it, under other conditions: a model reader in place of a person, any omitted task-state fact rather than what was not done or verified, and a changed instruction rather than a correct one, explicit targeting of the supplied fact found in 1 of 7 inspected instructions; Biswas & Lin 2026 found in 489,283 logged response pairs from ShareChat and WildChat that an uncertainty cue accompanied a user's checking slightly more than his challenging (direct ratio 1.06, 95% CI 1.02 to 1.10, q = 0.018), and in technical help by 1.26 (95% CI 1.17 to 1.34), an observation of cases as they came, in the claim's direction under other conditions: hedges in chat answers rather than a manipulated sentence, and checking rather than a correct action; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07, plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/, made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had operator-report-with-failing-build E10 (the report names what is not done: the performance run and the routing documentation) and operator-report-upgrade-that-silences-an-alert E9 (the story says what was not verified: no build or run) pass with the skill and pass without it, so the run showed no difference, and no expectation of the run checks that a gap which changes nothing is left out · beside-bounds: 300 clinicians, corresponding authors of trial reports, each reading one abstract of a systematic review online, 2013 to 2014; 404 US crowd workers on eight medical yes or no questions with a fictional LLM search engine, 2023; 192 participants on eight medical questions with fixed LLM answers, 2026; readers of science news reports and journalism students in a mock newsroom, 2019; a model supervisor over reports of coding agents on 37 repository tasks, 2026; logged conversations of public chat corpora, 2026
- **H-Explaining-a-value-the-story-states-is-one-the-author-can-point-at** · a story whose author traced each stated consequence of a change through every file behind the things it names, and in which every value stands on an anchored line under "Where to look" or is said in the first person to be computed or not verified, delivers fewer values that contradict the material behind them, and fewer wrong values its reader accepts, than the same story written without the rule · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of values stated in the delivered story that contradict the files, runs or computations behind them, and share of those wrong values the reader accepts as stated before he acts · against: the same story written without the rule: values stated with no anchor and no first-person mark, and the consequence of a change written without tracing it through the files behind the things named · scope: reports and explanations an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of stated values that contradict the material behind them is not lower with the rule than without it, or the reader accepts as large a share of the wrong values with the rule as without it · beside: external arXiv:2309.11495v2 Tables 1 and 3 first-hand; no test of our own exists yet; the eval run of 2026-11-07, plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/runs/iteration-2/, made before this prediction was recorded with 1 run per configuration, had operator-report-rename-and-a-stale-override E8 (the story says production would get 3000 ms where it had 10000 ms, graded by both numbers being named) pass with the skill and pass without it, a run that showed no difference · beside-bounds: Llama 65B and Llama 2 70B Chat on Wikidata and Wiki-Category list questions, closed-book MultiSpanQA and biographies, scored by precision and FactScore, 2023; - **H-Media-complete-without-the-sound-proof-in-place** · a page with voice whose whole content is readable at rest and whose list "Where to look" opens the lines around each anchor in place is understood better by a reader who does not start the voice, and has more of its claims checked or the same share checked in less time, than the same page with part of its content shown only while the voice plays and with bare path:line anchors · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: the score on questions about the page's matter of readers who do not start the voice, and the share of the list's claims a reader checks against their lines with his time per check · against: the same page with part of its content shown only while the voice plays it or spoken and not written, and its list "Where to look" as bare path:line anchors that the reader opens in his editor · scope: reports and explanations an agent delivers as a page with voice for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: readers who do not start the voice score no higher on the complete page than on the page whose content waits for the player, or with the lines opened in place the share of claims checked is lower, or it is not higher and the time per check is not lower · beside: external https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/EJ1403866.pdf § Meta-regression, Table 2 first-hand;
