- **H-Page-sizes-are-computed-text-of-14-px-that-fits-its-box** · a page with voice whose figures draw every span of a time line on one scale and set their text at 14 px in boxes computed to hold it with room on both sides has fewer labels out of their boxes and is read correctly by more of its readers, in no longer time, than the same page with its figure text at 12.5 px and its boxes and spans sized by eye · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of figure labels that run over their box or over another element when the page is rendered in a browser, and share of readers who after one reading of the page state correctly what its figures' labels say and which span of a time line is the longer and by about how much, with the time from opening the page to the answer · against: the same page with the same story and figures, its figure text at 12.5 px and the widths of its boxes and spans set by eye · scope: pages with voice an agent builds from its reports and explanations for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of labels out of their boxes is not lower with the computed sizes, or the share of readers answering correctly is not higher with them, or the time to the answer is longer with them · beside: external doi:10.1145/2858036.2858204 Results, Table 1 first-hand; external doi:10.14236/ewic/hci2008.23 §3.1, Table 2 first-hand; external doi:10.1016/j.apergo.2018.03.007 §3.3 first-hand; external arXiv:1907.02035v2 Experiment One first-hand; external arXiv:2609.00194v2 §4.2, §4.3, Tables 1, 2 and 11 first-hand · beside-result: Rello, Pielot & Marcos had 104 readers at the Universitat Pompeu Fabra, 93 of them analysed for fixations, read Wikipedia articles in Arial at 10, 12, 14, 18, 22 and 26 points on a 17-inch screen of 1024x768 pixels from about 60 cm, and the mean fixation duration fell from .255 at 10 points to .199 at 22 (two-way ANOVA F(5, 445) = 66.825, p < .001), each of 10, 12 and 14 points drawing longer fixations than every larger size in Holm-corrected t-tests, and comprehension scores were lower at 10 and 12 points than at 18 (Friedman test χ²(5) = 27.29, p < .001, Holm-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, p < .01) and at 12 than at 26 (p < .05), the source listing no difference in comprehension among 10, 12 and 14 points, in the claim's direction on larger text, with no comprehension gain shown among the three smallest sizes, under other conditions: running encyclopedia text with no box to fit rather than labels in a figure, and sizes from 10 points up, set through the style sheet on a 17-inch screen of 1024x768 pixels on which a CSS unit is physically larger than on current screens, the paper not saying whether its points are CSS points, rather than 12.5 against 14 px, about 9.4 and 10.5 CSS points; Beymer, Russell & Orton gave 82 employees of a computer company one science story in Verdana at 10, 12 or 14 pt, between subjects, with a three-question retention test, and fixations were longer at 10 than at 14 pt (281 against 239 ms, F(1,53) = 25.6, p < 0.00001) while first-pass reading was not significantly faster at 14 pt (12.6% faster, F(1,53) = 2.845) and retention was 89.2, 90.1 and 88.9 % correct at 10, 12 and 14 pt, the source naming no test of retention, in the claim's direction on fixations and with no difference on speed and retention, under other conditions: a page of running text rather than labels in a figure; Dobres, Wolfe, Chahine & Reimer had 30 adults aged 35 to 75 decide whether a string in a crowded array, set in Frutiger with a capital H 3 or 4 mm high, was a word, the display time adjusted by a staircase to 79.4% accurate responses, and the larger text needed less display time (repeated-measures ANOVA F(1, 29) = 24.54, p < 0.001), in the claim's direction on larger text, under other conditions: words read at a glance in white on black on a 27-inch monitor at 68.58 cm rather than labels read at the reader's own pace, and a step from 3 to 4 mm rather than from 12.5 to 14 px; Correll, Bertini & Franconeri had 40 crowd-sourced participants, one excluded, rate on a five-point scale how different the values were in 48 bar and line charts whose axis began at 0, 25 or 50%, and the same differences were rated more severe the further the axis began from zero (repeated-measures ANOVA F(2, 76) = 89, p < 0.0001, an increase of 0.36 for an axis starting at 25% rather than 0%), in the claim's direction on drawing lengths to one scale, under other conditions: an axis cut below the values of a data chart rather than spans of one time line drawn to different scales, and a rated severity rather than a reading of durations; Tian, Zeng, Yang et al. had slide agents build decks from 100 papers with three seeds, and on GPT-5.4 the clean-slide rate, the share of a deck's slides a vision judge found free of clipped text, overlapping elements, elements off the canvas and clipped images, averaged over the decks and seeds, was 64.1 for the first draft, 68.2 after a critique in words per turn, 78.4 after a screenshot critique per turn, 80.2 with the measured layout returned after every edit alone and 91.5 with that feedback, a critic per turn and a gate at submission, the source giving standard errors of 0.8 to 1.7 for the ablation cells and naming no test among them, while the whole system, its own first draft and loop, against DeepPresenter's first draft and its loop that inspects rendered slides gained 24.7 points (task-bootstrap 95% CI 21.5 to 27.9, paired permutation tests significant after Holm correction), and from a shared first draft its loop gained 19.5 and 20.2 points over DeepPresenter's, with 95% CIs the source reports as excluding zero, and on Claude-4.6 the rate rose from 62.5 to 89.6 in a replication the source calls descriptive, a tested finding the claim does not cover, on layout measured and returned after each edit rather than on 14 px text in boxes computed before drawing against 12.5 px set by eye, under other conditions: slides built from papers rather than figures of a page with voice, and no reader measured · beside-bounds: Rello et al.: 104 readers, 92 of them studying at a university or graduated from one, Wikipedia articles on animals in Arial, a Tobii 1750 with a 17-inch screen at about 60 cm, end of 2012; - **H-Media-every-part-takes-the-reader-s-words** · a page with voice that puts a field for the reader's words after each part and carries them into the copied answer one line a part brings the session his remarks on more of the parts, each tied to the part it concerns, with no more follow-up rounds and no loss of understanding, than the same page with the decision's field alone · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: number of parts on which the reader's remarks reach the session in the copied answer, share of those remarks a second reader ties to the part they concern, follow-up messages the session sends before it acts on them, and share of questions on the content the reader answers correctly after one reading · against: the same page and voice with a field under the decisions only, the reader writing his remarks on the parts there or in the chat after reading · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, delivered as a page with voice, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the field after every part the reader's remarks reach the session on no more parts, or tied to their part no more often, than with the decision's field alone, or the session sends as many follow-up messages, or the reader answers fewer questions on the content correctly · beside: external doi:10.18148/srm/2025.v19i2.8295 § 5, Tables 3, 4 and 7 first-hand; - **H-Explaining-the-name-is-the-reader-s** · an explanation or report that names each thing by the word the reader's trade says for it aloud, an English term or abbreviation kept in Latin letters in a Russian or German text with its expansion and one sentence of meaning at its first mention, and otherwise by the code's name as its authors say it, is retold correctly by more of its readers, and has fewer of its terms replaced by them, than the same text with coined native words or literal translations in place of those terms · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers whose retelling after one reading states the mechanism correctly, and the number of the text's terms the readers replace with words of their own · against: the same text with each term of the trade or of the code replaced by a coined native word or a literal translation · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of correct retellings with the trade's terms is not above the share with the coined words, or the readers replace as many terms of the version with the trade's terms as of the version with the coined words · beside: external arXiv:2502.17882v2 §4, §5, Fig. 4 first-hand; external doi:10.1016/S0749-596X(03)00028-7 Results, Table 2 first-hand; external doi:10.1145/3051457.3051464 Table 3 first-hand; external doi:10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2022.12320 Results first-hand; external https://www.psychologicalscience.org/observer/alienating-the-audience-how-abbreviations-hamper-scientific-communication § Abbreviations as Alienating first-hand · beside-result: Kleidermacher & Zou gave 15 researchers a GPT-4o translation of their own paper into their native language, and 33.3% commented that technical terms were overtranslated; five authors who had raised concerns about the translation of terms then compared a direct translation of an excerpt with one that kept some English terms (15.6% of its words on average), and three of five preferred the version with the English terms and two the complete translation, the source naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: authors' stated preference for a translation of their own paper rather than a reader's retelling of an agent's explanation, and English terms kept by a model prompted with one example rather than by the trade's spoken use; Metzing & Brennan had 24 undergraduates settle with a speaker on a name for an object, and when that speaker switched to a new name they touched the object 540 ms later than when a new speaker used the new name (planned contrast t(23) = 2.30, p = .031), in 2595 ms against 1897 ms when the original speaker kept the name, in the claim's direction for one name per thing, under other conditions: a name set up in the task rather than the trade's term, spoken reference to objects, and the time to touch rather than a retelling; Dasgupta & Hill compared, in longitudinal observational data on 15,015 Scratch users of five countries, users whose programming keywords and interface were in their home country's language with users of the same countries on the English interface, and the users of the localized interface began with fewer blocks (main effect β = −0.056, SE = 0.015, p < 0.01) while their repertoire grew faster with shared projects (interaction β = 0.027, SE = 0.004, p < 0.001; at 16 shared projects a predicted repertoire of 37.9 against 37.1), an effect the authors call small and read as correlation, not causation, against the claim under other conditions: young novices learning to program rather than engineers of the trade or a client reading a report, a fully translated interface of website, editor and blocks rather than a trade's term kept in Latin letters and expanded at its first mention against a coined word, the growth of a block repertoire in observational data rather than a retelling after one reading or the terms readers replace, and the language chosen by the users rather than assigned; Grossman Liu et al. randomized 60 patients with heart failure, 30 per arm, to clinical text with 10 common abbreviations or with their expansions, and comprehension was 9.5 of 10 with the expansions against 6.2 (95% CI 9.3 to 9.7 against 5.3 to 7.0, P < .001), significant only for moderately difficult terms, in the claim's direction for the expansion of an abbreviation, under other conditions: lay readers for whom the abbreviation is not their own word, and the expansion in place of the abbreviation rather than beside it at its first mention; Hales, Williams & Rector randomly assigned 98 college students to a recruitment message whose abbreviations were spelled out on every mention, clarified on the first mention only, or never clarified, and as the passage became less clear the students reported more difficulty reading it, the article giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction for an abbreviation clarified at its first mention against one never clarified, and for readers outside the group against keeping the abbreviation where the spelled-out words can stand, under other conditions: students reading a fitness organization's message, and self-reported difficulty rather than a retelling · beside-bounds: 15 researchers of quantum optics, nanophotonics, biostatistics, materials science, magneto-electronics, machine learning and other fields reading GPT-4o translations of their own papers into native languages among them Korean, French, Chinese, Italian, Spanish and German, five of them in the follow-up, a preprint of 2025; 24 native English-speaking undergraduates at Stony Brook in a spoken matching task with a confederate, 2003; 15,015 Scratch users of Portugal, Italy, Brazil, Germany and Norway, longitudinal observational data, mixed-effects Poisson regression, 2017; 60 English-speaking adult patients with heart failure at 3 US hospitals, enrolled February 2020 to August 2021, 10 common abbreviations in clinical text; 98 college students reading a CrossFit recruitment message, self-reported reading difficulty, 2017
- **H-Explaining-a-computed-quantity-is-said-by-its-purpose-before-its-formula** · a report or explanation that says what a computed quantity is for and what happens without it before it gives the quantity's formula is understood by more of its readers than the same text that gives the formula first · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who, after one reading, say correctly what the quantity is for and how it moves when one of its inputs changes · against: the same text with the same formula given first and the sentences of purpose after it or left out · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share with the purpose before the formula is not higher than with the formula first · beside: external https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED207592.pdf §2.6 Table 4 and §2.7 Table 6 first-hand; external doi:10.1037/0096-3445.127.4.355 Experiment 1, Table 4 first-hand; external doi:10.1037/a0025140 Experiment 1 first-hand; external doi:10.1111/bjep.12035 abstract · beside-result: ED207592 §2.6 and §2.7: novices were given a concrete model of what the computer's units do before a manual of BASIC-like statements or after it, 20 subjects per group on a test of six problem types and 30 per group on recall, and solved more creative-transfer problems with the model before (generation-looping .20 against .13, interpretation-statement .47 against .27, interpretation-nonloop .63 against .40) and more retention-like problems with it after (generation-statement .57 against .77, generation-nonloop .50 against .63), and recalled more conceptual idea units with it before (6.6 against 4.9 of 35) and more technical (5.0 against 6.0 of 14) and format units (1.9 against 2.9 of 12) with it after, an interaction of group and problem type at p < .05 in each and no main effect of the order reported, the source naming no test for a single cell, in the claim's direction on transfer and on the meaning recalled, which the claim's measure counts, the lead of the group shown the model after the manual lying on problems like the manual's and on the recall of the code and its grammar, which the claim does not measure, under other conditions: an analogy of the whole machine placed before or after a programming manual rather than a sentence of what one computed quantity is for placed before its formula; doi:10.1037/0096-3445.127.4.355 Experiment 1: 150 college students studied three Poisson worked examples in which the steps that sum the total frequency carried a label naming that total in the problem's words, an abstract label or no label, and solved on average 2.88, 1.84 and 1.04 of four novel problems, an analysis of variance giving label type F(2, 144) = 13.60, p < .0001, the meaningful label beating no label at p = .004 among students with a stronger mathematics background and beating both other groups at p < .0005 among those with a weaker one, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a label saying what a group of steps computes, set beside the steps, against no label, rather than the purpose of a quantity said before its formula against after it; doi:10.1037/a0025140 Experiment 1: 128 eighth-graders were told the concept and the formula of density and speed before practicing on contrasting cases, or invented an index from the same cases and were told afterwards, both groups used the formulas on word problems equally well, and the inventing group transferred more to a spring-constant problem three weeks later, F(1,124) = 13.6, p < .001, d = .33, under other conditions: inventing a formula from the problem it solves against being told it first, while the told group too read the concept before the formula; doi:10.1111/bjep.12035: 122 second- and third-grade children were randomly given conceptual instruction on math equivalence before or after solving and explaining equivalence problems with feedback, and instruction first gave more procedural and conceptual knowledge, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: the meaning of the equal sign taught before or after problem solving rather than the purpose of a quantity said before or after its formula · beside-bounds: ED207592: novices reading at their own rate a 10-page manual of BASIC-like statements, the model a diagram of the computer's four units with a one-page description, 20 and 30 subjects per group, a 1981 report of the author's experiments published in 1976 and 1980; doi:10.1037/0096-3445.127.4.355: 150 students of Atlanta-area colleges with no prior probability course, 50 per condition, three Poisson worked examples and four novel problems, 1998; doi:10.1037/a0025140: 128 eighth-graders of four classes at one middle school, four lessons on density and speed, a transfer problem three weeks later, 2011; doi:10.1111/bjep.12035: 122 second- and third-grade children, mathematical equivalence, 2014

against the text, Mann–Whitney U = 323.5, p = 0.047, U = 306.0, p = 0.023, and U = 221.5, p = 0.003, with Bonferroni correction), the interactive formats not differing significantly from one another (p > 0.05), so the test does not tell the graph from the interaction, the colour-coded variables, the problem summary and the step playback the interactive formats shared, a finding outside the claim's comparison, under other conditions: interactive views of a model's solution steps for grade-school math rather than a picture of a recorded run beside a story; - **H-Explainer-the-genre-its-signs-and-what-differs-from-a-report** · for matter that several readers return to with different questions and that waits for no decision of theirs, an explanation with parts bounded by the questions they answer, a table of questions and routes by time to enter it, the list of places in story order and reference material in tables the voice does not read lets each reader answer his own questions from it correctly and sooner, and leaves fewer later questions the text already answers, than the same matter written as a report · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of each reader's own questions about the mechanism that he answers correctly from the text, his time to each answer, and the share of the questions the readers ask the author after reading whose answer the text already holds · against: the same matter written as a report by the skill's report rules: one opening, one length bound for the whole text, a list of where to look of five lines, no table of questions and no routes on the page · scope: explanations of a mechanism that an agent writes for several human readers, the team that builds on it, reviews it or needs its gist, delivered as a page with voice, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the readers answer as large a share of their own questions correctly and as fast from the report as from the explanation, or they ask the author as large a share of questions already answered by the text after the explanation as after the report · beside: external arXiv:1704.00074 §5.3 H2b and H3a first-hand; arXiv:2305.17286v1: 65 senior undergraduate and graduate students answered three architecture questions on one system from a narrative essay or from a structured Views-and-Beyond document holding the same information, and a Bayesian ordered categorical regression gave the structured format coefficients of -0.38 [-1.05, 0.27] on Q2, -1.03 [-1.78, -0.30] on Q3 and -1.04 [-1.84, -0.31] on Q4, the text naming an influence of the format on Q3 alone and the abstract none, while prior familiarity with the source code raised the score on all three, a neighbouring question: a narrative against a structured reference document rather than an explanation against a report; doi:10.1207/s1532690xci1303_4: an electronic text with a structured overview or a menu-like content listing against the same continuous text, both overviews gave better memory for the text's topics and broader recall, and the menu-like one a more fragmented knowledge structure when readers lacked specific study goals, the abstract giving no figures and naming no test, in the claim's direction on entry by an overview and against it for readers without a question of their own, under other conditions: an instructional text and recall rather than an explanation of a mechanism and answers to the readers' own questions; doi:10.1080/10494820.2025.2604649: 29 graduate students categorising papers with structural AI summarisation, conversational AI summarisation or none, structural summarisation helped them navigate the text's structure and locate information at lower perceived cognitive load than conversational summarisation, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction on entry by structure, under other conditions: a summary beside academic papers rather than an explanation's table of questions, and categorising papers rather than answering questions about a mechanism; arXiv:2605.19174v2: 14 newcomers assigned at random to an open-source project's contributing documentation restructured by a model into task-based units with workflows and diagrams, or to the original documentation with ChatGPT allowed, completed 20 of 21 tasks against 13 of 21 (Fisher's exact test p = 0.02), in the claim's direction, under other conditions: procedural onboarding tasks rather than questions about a mechanism, and a restructured tool against the original document rather than an explanation against a report; - **H-Explainer-the-registry-as-the-first-table-routes-by-time-a-part-per-named-questions-the-table-of-terms-reference-tables-without-voice** · an explanation page for several readers that opens with routes by time and a table of the readers' questions, each with a one-sentence answer and a link to the part that answers it, sets a table of terms before the first part and gives reference material as tables the voice does not read lets a reader of the team answer his own questions about the mechanism correctly more often and sooner, a part entered from the table included, than the same parts read as one story from the start · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the reader's own questions about the mechanism he answers correctly from the page and the minutes to each answer, and share of comprehension questions on a part entered from the table and read with the table of terms that he answers correctly · against: the same parts in the same words read as one story from the start, without the routes, the table of questions and the table of terms and with the reference tables read by the voice; for a part entered from the table, the same part read after the parts before it · scope: explanations an agent writes for several human readers, the team of the operator of a coding session, delivered as a page with voice, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the routes and the tables the share of questions answered correctly is not above the share without them and the answers take no less time, or a part entered from the table is answered correctly less often than the same part read after the parts before it · beside: external arXiv:2203.00130v1 §6.1, §7.2–7.3, Table 2 first-hand; external arXiv:2009.14237v3 §6, Table 2 first-hand; external doi:10.1207/s15326950dp3102_03 Experiment 2 abstract; external PMID 17238339 Table 2 first-hand; external the review of the explanation page by four of its readers (research/2026-11-22_explainer-review-evidence.md § The review, the record kept by the repository owner) relay · beside-result: arXiv:2203.00130v1: 24 Upwork workers without medical training each read two medical papers for 10 minutes with two of four interfaces, and the variant with the key question index and answer gists alone (8 curated questions, each listed with the first 1–2 sentences of a GPT-3 gist and linked to the highlighted answering paragraph) against a typical PDF reader moved self-rated reading difficulty by −1.639, understanding by 1.281 and confidence of having got all relevant information by 1.361 points on scales of 1 to 5, mixed-effects models with Holm-Bonferroni-corrected p = 0.0003, 0.0008 and 0.0006, in the claim's direction on self-reports, while section gists with term definitions against the PDF reader gave −0.498, 0.457 and 0.415 (p = 0.4786, 0.4106, 0.5093), no difference; on 7 multiple-choice questions a paper, written so that the key questions and gists could not answer them, the full interface scored 3.67 against 3.50 for the PDF reader (non-inferiority t-test t(28) = 1.82, p < 0.05), a test that can show that comprehension did not fall, with no test of a rise run and questions built so that a gain from the gists could not show, and the text gives no comprehension figure for the key question index alone, which only Figure 10 plots; under other conditions: a fixed list of curated questions rather than the readers' own, lay readers of medical papers with GPT-3 gists rather than a team reading an explanation of a mechanism, no routes and no voice, self-rated measures, and a fixed reading time with no time to an answer; arXiv:2009.14237v3: 27 researchers answered three timed questions on one paper, one with each of three interfaces, and the full interface, with definitions of terms and symbols in tooltips, lists, equation diagrams and a priming glossary, against a basic PDF reader took 45.4 s less (p = 0.0001) at no difference in correct answers (0 points, p = 1.000), a GLMM with Holm-Bonferroni correction, in the claim's direction on time and showing no difference on correct answers, under other conditions: definitions shown at the term rather than in a table before the first part, and the priming glossary, consulted for 10 s or more by 6 of 27 in free reading, not separated from the other features; doi:10.1207/s15326950dp3102_03: in Experiment 2 a structured overview (table of contents) facilitated information localization and did not influence the summary of undergraduates who searched a 2,000-word text on atomic models to answer questions, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction on locating, under other conditions: a table of contents without answers, students and a science text; PMID 17238339: one reader rated 65 technical medical sentences 2.23 of 5 for comprehensibility as written and 3.72 with automatically mined definitions of unfamiliar terms inserted at their first mention (Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney p < 0.0001), the versions shown together to the one rater, in the claim's direction under other conditions: definitions inside the sentence rather than in a table, a rating of comprehensibility rather than answers, and one rater; the review of the explanation page: on the page built with the routes, the table of questions and the table of terms before its first part, four of its readers could not rebuild the algorithm and went to the code, and at least six of the questions they asked had their answer on the page, as the owner's summary of the meeting says, against the claim, under other conditions: the same parts were not read as one story without the tables, so the comparison the claim names was not made, and the record of the meeting was not opened in this repository · beside-bounds: 24 college-educated Upwork workers without medical training reading two medical papers with GPT-3 gists, within subjects, 2022; - **H-Explainer-a-question-after-a-part-a-hidden-answer-that-explains-questions-that-ask-to-use-the-mechanism-no-count** · readers of an explanation page whose explaining parts each close with a question that asks to use the part's mechanism, its answer hidden until opened and giving the cause and the number, with no score shown, later answer more new questions on those mechanisms correctly than readers of the same page without the questions, and each of the three properties adds to that share · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of correct answers, a day or more after the reading, to new questions that ask to apply the mechanisms of the page's parts, beside the share of parts the readers mark and later return to · against: the same page with no question after its parts; and the same questions with one property changed: a hidden answer that gives only the verdict or the right answer without its cause, a question that asks a fact of the part back, or a count of the reader's right answers shown on the page · scope: explanations an agent writes as a page with voice for several human readers, the team of the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: the share of correct answers with the questions is not above the share without them by a test that tells the difference from chance, or changing any one of the three properties leaves the share as high or higher · beside: external doi:10.3102/00346543056002212 Tables VI, VIII, IX and XII first-hand; external doi:10.1080/00461520.2013.775712 abstract · beside-result: Hamaker pooled adjunct-question experiments published between 1965 and 1983 and found that factual questions placed after a passage, against the same text read without them, raised the proportion of correct criterion answers by .16 on repeated items (59 outcomes, t(58) = 12.21), by .07 on related items (t(33) = 5.50, p < .01) and by .01 on unrelated ones (t(49) = .75, p > .20), on unrelated ones by .03 when study time was free (t(30) = 2.92, p < .01) and by -.03 when it was fixed (t(15) = -1.69, p > .10), and that higher order questions, against factual ones, raised repeated higher order items by .15 (t(8) = 3.85, p < .01), related ones by .04 (t(30) = 1.66, p < .10, one-tailed), unrelated ones by .05 (t(14) = 3.21, p < .01) and unrelated factual items by .03 (t(17) = 1.54, p > .10), while factual questions beat higher order ones on repeated factual items by .09 (t(16) = -2.29, p < .05) and on related factual items by .06 (t(9) = -1.41, p < .10, one-tailed), in the claim's direction for the question after a part and for the question that asks to use, under other conditions: students instructed to answer questions on instructional prose with no hidden answer named, not readers of a page who may pass a question by, and, among the outcomes of postquestion studies that tested once, most from a test given at once rather than a day or more later, though effect sizes did not seem to depend on the delay; Alreshidi & Alrashidi randomly assigned 126 mathematics and English teachers to a higher-order multiple-choice question, with feedback that told why a chosen answer was wrong without revealing the right one, in place of the stated interpretation of each of four analogical paintings, against the interpretation given, and the question group scored 8.42 against 6.94 on a knowledge-transfer post-test (ANCOVA F(1, 123) = 13.271, p < .001, d = 0.66), in the claim's direction for a question that asks to use, under other conditions: the question replaced the stated content rather than following it, and the feedback withheld the answer; An, Liu, Acharya & Hashmi compared quiz scores of about 60 students in two college data science courses after a week with LLM-generated multiple-choice retrieval questions against a week without, 89% against 73% (Mann-Whitney U = 703, p < 0.0001, rank-biserial r = 0.586, 31 and 27 quizzes), in the claim's direction for the question after a part, under other conditions: a quasi-experiment across two weeks of different material, a preprint; Agarwal found that retrieval practice raised delayed test performance over rereading or no quizzes and that higher order and mixed quizzes raised higher order test performance while fact quizzes did not, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction for the question after a part and the question that asks to use, under other conditions: middle school and college students on course material; Van der Kleij, Feskens & Eggen found over 70 effect sizes from 40 studies of item-based feedback in computer-based environments elaborated feedback, such as an explanation, at 0.49 against 0.32 for the correct answer and 0.05 for right or wrong, the abstract naming no test for these differences, in the claim's direction for the answer that explains, under other conditions: students in computer-based practice; Wisniewski, Zierer & Hattie found over 435 studies (k = 994) high-information feedback at d = 0.99 [0.82, 1.15] against 0.46 [0.39, 0.55] for corrective feedback and 0.24 [0.06, 0.43] for reinforcement or punishment, feedback type being a moderator with QB = 41.52 (df 2, p < 0.0001), in the claim's direction for the answer that explains, under other conditions: high-information feedback adds information on self-regulation to the corrective kind rather than the cause of the answer, in educational settings; Butler, Godbole & Marsh gave subjects who had studied prose passages correct-answer feedback, explanation feedback or none after an initial short-answer test, and two days later explanation feedback produced superior performance on new inference questions and equivalent performance on repeated ones, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction for the answer that explains against the correct answer alone, under other conditions: feedback after a test on prose passages; Asher, Cao & Carvalho assigned 400 adults practising constraint equations to the correct answer alone, a generic explanation, a personalized LLM explanation or Socratic tutoring in a preregistered experiment, and on a five-item posttest given immediately after the session the three explaining conditions pooled against the correct answer alone scored 0.19 SD higher on the practice-aligned questions (t(392) = 1.68, p = .094), 0.06 SD lower on the pretest-aligned question (t(392) = -0.52, p = .603) and 0.15 SD lower on the challenge question (t(392) = -1.33, p = .185), no difference significant and the preregistered hypothesis that explaining the answer helps beyond stating it not supported, while Socratic tutoring scored 0.24 SD above the two explanations on practice-aligned questions (t(392) = 1.98, p = .048) and took 13.3 minutes against 6.8 to 7.3, no detectable gain for the answer that explains when the right answer is shown in both arms, which bounds the size of a gain without showing the correct answer alone as high or higher, under other conditions: feedback after the learner's own attempts at equation problems practised online rather than a question after a part of a page, a posttest given at once rather than a day or more later, and Socratic tutoring pooled with the two explanations in the contrast; Cao, Zhao, Schunn, McLaughlin, Lin & Koedinger randomly assigned 199 college students recruited online to LLM feedback that gave the correct answer with its explanation at once or feedback that first encouraged and hinted and revealed the answer later, and the first scored 11.24 against 10.40 of 20 adjusted post-test points (ANCOVA contrast t(196) = 2.32, p = .02), in the claim's direction for an answer that explains at once, under other conditions: the comparison is with encouragement and hints before the answer rather than with a bare verdict, one chapter on the multimedia principle, a preprint; Kluger & DeNisi found over the 470 effects of feedback interventions left after all exclusions feedback that gave the correct solution at d = .43 against .25 without it, praise at .09 against .34 and the top quartile of threat to self-esteem at .08 against .47 in the bottom one, each moderator judged against a Type I error of .01, two-tailed, and no effect for normative feedback, in the claim's direction for the answer and for the count, under other conditions: laboratory and field tasks of many kinds, and praise and threat to self-esteem rather than a count of one's own right answers; Butler randomly assigned 12 classes of fifth and sixth graders to numerical grades, individual comments or both after thinking tasks, and among 132 pupils scores at the third session were higher after comments than after grades or grades plus comments (F(1, 125) = 98.68 on the convergent task and 160.1 on the divergent one, p < 0.001), grades plus comments doing no better than grades alone and worse on the convergent task (F(1, 125) = 11.8, p < 0.001), in the claim's direction for the count beside an explaining comment, under other conditions: grades given to children on thinking tasks, not a count of one's own right answers; Koenka, Linnenbrink-Garcia, Moshontz, Atkinson, Sanchez & Cooper found in four meta-analyses of elementary and secondary school students that grades, against comments, went with poorer achievement and less optimal motivation and, against no feedback, with higher achievement and lower motivation, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction for the count against comments and against it against no feedback, under other conditions: grades given in school; Say, Visentin, Saunders, Atherton, Carr & King found in a mixed-methods study of nursing students, its quantitative data from surveys, test results, focus groups and discussion-board posts read in a quasi-experimental design and its qualitative data analysed thematically, that a score alone after formative online multiple-choice tests, against more directive feedback, went with more cognitive engagement with the content, constructive collaboration, richer sources sought and lower satisfaction, the abstract giving no figure, no sample size, no test and no test-score result, against the claim for the answer that explains on a neighbouring measure only, engagement rather than correct answers to new questions a day or more later, and bearing on the count neither way, since the abstract does not say whether the directive arm saw a score, under other conditions: a score in place of the explanation rather than beside it; Alfieri, Nokes-Malach & Schunn found over 57 experiments with 336 tests that case comparison led to more learning than sequential, single-case or nonanalogous study and traditional instruction (d = 0.50, 95% CI [0.44, 0.56]), more when learners were asked to find similarities, the principle came after the comparison and the test came at once, in the claim's direction for the question that sets the source method against its use here, under other conditions: learners in laboratory and classroom studies of case comparison · beside-bounds: Hamaker: adjunct-question experiments with students reading instructional prose, published 1965 to 1983, outcomes as proportions correct, criterion tests given immediately or after a delay of a few minutes to more than 2 weeks; Alreshidi & Alrashidi: 126 mathematics and English teachers, four paintings with explanatory text on principles of learning, online forms, 2026; An et al.: about 60 students of two college data science courses, multiple-choice questions over two-week cycles, 2025; Agarwal: middle school and college students in laboratory and classroom settings, 2019; Van der Kleij et al.: 40 studies of item-based feedback in computer-based learning environments, 2015; Wisniewski et al.: 435 studies of educational feedback, k = 994, N > 61,000, 2020; Butler, Godbole & Marsh: two experiments with prose passages, short-answer tests and a final test two days later, 2013; Asher et al.: 400 adults online who could not yet set up a basic constraint equation, practising constraint equations with feedback after each attempt, a five-item posttest immediately after the session, 2026; Cao et al.: 199 college students recruited online, one chapter on the multimedia principle, LLM-generated feedback, 2026; Kluger & DeNisi: 607 effects from 131 papers, laboratory and field studies of tasks of many kinds, 1996; Butler: 132 fifth and sixth graders in Israel, convergent and divergent thinking tasks, 1988; Koenka et al.: elementary and secondary school students, 2021; Say et al.: nursing students, formative online multiple-choice tests, a mixed-methods study with a quasi-experimental design for its quantitative data, 2023; Alfieri et al.: 57 laboratory and classroom experiments on case comparison, 2013
- **H-Explainer-where-to-look-in-story-order-given-as-data-and-resolved-by-fragment** · an explanation page whose list of where to look names every place its story rests on, in the story's order, and is built from data — what the line proves, the file and a fragment of the line, resolved at each build to the one line that holds it and refused when it stands on no line or on several — keeps more of its anchors at the line that bears their claim after code edits that move lines, shows fewer at a wrong line without notice, and lets its readers check more of the story's claims than the same page built from path:line anchors typed by the author and listing only the five places the story turns on · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: the share of the page's anchors that show the line bearing their claim after the code they cite is edited and the page rebuilt, the share shown at a wrong line with no refusal, and the share of the story's claims a reader of the team checks correctly against the code from the list · against: the same page built from path:line anchors typed by the author, or from the same fragments resolved to the first line that holds them; for the readers, the same page whose list names only the five places the story turns on · scope: explanation pages an agent builds with this skill's builder for several human readers, a team that maintains the code the page cites, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: after edits that move lines the page built from fragments shows no larger share of its anchors at the line bearing their claim than the page with typed path:line anchors, or as large a share at a wrong line without a refusal as the build that takes the first match; or readers check no larger share of the story's claims correctly with the full list in the story's order than with the five-place list · beside: external arXiv:2511.05205v1 §3.3, §3.4.2, Tables 2 and 3, Figure 11 first-hand; external doi:10.1109/ICSM.2013.34 §IV, Table II first-hand; external doi:10.1145/1368088.1368091 abstract; external Microsoft Research MSR-TR-2001-107 §3, §5, Table 1 first-hand; external arXiv:2501.01303v1 Results, Supplement C Table S5 first-hand · beside-result: arXiv:2511.05205v1: a tool that joins git diff candidates, movement detection and an exact search for the region's text, against line-level and word-level git diff, found the exact target region in 77.0% against 43.0% and 54.0% of 100 annotated pairs (Data A), 71.0% against 44.0% and 54.0% of another 100 (Data B), 80.7% against 21.9% and 21.9% of 187 Python suppressions, and 75.7%, 81.8% and 94.5% against at most 42.6%, 78.6% and 93.0% of 2,005 Java CodeTracker pairs, disabling the text search lowered F1 from 0.898 to 0.867 on Data A, from 0.882 to 0.755 on Data B, from 0.827 to 0.552 on the suppression data and from 0.879 to 0.594 on the CodeTracker data, and 75 of 219 wrong targets were coincidental occurrences of the source region at unrelated locations, the source names no test for these comparisons, in the claim's direction for finding a line by its text after the code changes and for the risk of a text that stands in several places, under other conditions: a tool mapping a region between two commits scored against a hand-annotated target, with git diff and not a kept line number as the baseline, and no reader; doi:10.1109/ICSM.2013.34: on the Reiss, Eclipse and NetBeans benchmarks LHDiff, which matches a line by its content and its context, mapped 97.0%, 82.8% and 85.5% of tracked lines correctly, Reiss's W_BESTI_LINE, which also matches by content and context, 96.7%, 52.6% and 61.9%, and diff 92.5%, 41.0% and 48.4%, the source names no test, in the claim's direction for locating a line by its content on the Eclipse benchmark, the one whose files the source calls heavily edited, though content and context alone did not do it there for W_BESTI_LINE; and a finding the claim does not cover: on the Eclipse and NetBeans benchmarks 99.3% and 98.6% of diff's errors reported a line deleted that still stands, close to a refusal, while LHDiff's errors included a line mapped to a wrong place (22.5% and 18.6%) and a deleted line mapped (20% and 13.6%), on Eclipse about 7% of all its lines, so its gain in lines found comes with lines shown at a wrong place, under other conditions: line trackers scored on benchmarks of source files, with no kept line number among the techniques and no reader; doi:10.1145/1368088.1368091: an experiment that tracks a variety of locations over the evolution of a source file, the abstract reporting that relatively simple techniques can be very effective with no figure and no test, so its direction on line numbers against content cannot be read from it; MSR-TR-2001-107: 8 adults annotated an article and rated on a 7-point scale where their 115 annotations were placed in a version a colleague had edited, by keyword anchoring or by a search for the annotated text and its shortened forms, and rated keyword anchoring higher (medians 6.5 against 5.5 and 5.0, Friedman χ2(3, N = 8) = 9.813, p = .02, the Wilcoxon follow-up under 0.05 for the version that places every anchor and under 0.1 for the one that orphans low scores), in the claim's direction for anchoring by content, while orphaning low-scoring anchors left the median at 6.5 and preference for an orphan with a guess over a placed anchor varied dramatically among participants, so the test does not tell a refusal from a guess, under other conditions: readers' own highlights and notes on prose rather than an author's anchors on code, in a technical report not reviewed by peers; arXiv:2501.01303v1: participants shown a chatbot's answers with five web citations checked at least one citation, 59 of 101 participants against 24 of 96 shown one citation (χ2(1, N = 197) = 21.19, p < 0.0001), in the claim's direction for a fuller list drawing more checks, and rated five citations no more trustworthy than one (p > .05), under other conditions: lay participants judging a chatbot's answers to their own questions with web links, the checks counted and their correctness not · beside-bounds: arXiv:2511.05205v1: 200 hand-annotated region pairs from 20 GitHub projects in ten languages at commit distances of up to five, 187 changes of Python suppressions and 2,005 Java CodeTracker pairs, a preprint of 2025 for ICSE 2026; - **H-Media-the-part-buttons-behind-one-control-past-six-parts** · on a page with voice of more than six parts, part buttons that open from one control let the reader begin the story sooner and start a part named to him no less often than the same page with every part button shown at its top · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: seconds from opening the page to the reader's first start of the voice or first sentence read, and share of readers who start a part named to them from its button · against: the same page of seven to twelve parts with every part button shown at its top · scope: pages with voice of more than six parts that an agent builds with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code for a human reader, the operator of a coding session, a client or a team · refuter: with the buttons behind one control the reader begins the story no sooner, or starts a named part less often than with the buttons shown · beside: external https://www.nngroup.com/articles/hamburger-menus/ § Navigation Usage and § Hidden Navigation Leads to Worse User Experience first-hand; - **H-Media-the-page-is-added-for-a-mechanism-a-figure-shows-or-a-decision-among-options** · a report that holds a mechanism a figure shows or a decision among options, whether or not the options behave differently on one input, has its mechanism understood and its decision answered correctly by its reader more often when the page with voice is added to the text than when the text goes out alone · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of questions on the report's mechanism the reader answers correctly after reading it, and share of its decisions he answers correctly in the first round with no question back, counted apart for options that behave differently on one input and for options that do not · against: the same report sent as the text alone, its pictures drawn as pseudographics · scope: reports and explanations an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: with the page added neither share is higher than with the text alone, or the share of decisions answered correctly is higher only where the options behave differently on one input · beside: external doi:10.1177/0272989X20960436 § Results, Effects of Modality first-hand; Synnot et al. 2014 pooled two real clinical trials that added audio-visual information to written or verbal consent information against that information alone, and knowledge of the trial did not differ (SMD 0.04, 95% CI −0.30 to 0.38, 143 participants, test for overall effect Z = 0.24, P = 0.81), no difference, while of two other real trials that counted participants with adequate knowledge, not pooled for their heterogeneity (I² = 99%), one favoured the added audio-visual information (82/100 against 30/100, RR 2.73, 95% CI 2.0 to 3.74) and one showed no difference (40/42 against 42/48, RR 1.09, 95% CI 0.96 to 1.24), and that second trial found more participants with adequate knowledge two to four weeks later (36/41 against 28/44, RR 1.38, 95% CI 1.07 to 1.77), which the review reads as audio-visual information added probably improving knowledge for at least two weeks, in the claim's direction, under other conditions: patients or guardians deciding whether to take part in a clinical trial, one decision to enter or not, and audio-visual presentations of trials published 1988 to 2012; - **H-Explaining-a-wrong-model-is-named-before-it-is-corrected** · two meta-analyses found that texts stating a common misconception before the explanation taught more and left more accurate beliefs than the same content without refutation, and short myth corrections for lay readers gained nothing from naming the myth, but no test compared a part that names one reader's own model and puts the yes or no after it with the same explanation and the yes or no first · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of readers who, after one reading, give a correct account of the mechanism and of what the thing they asked about gives or costs, or their score on a test of it, and the share who still hold the wrong model · against: the same part with the same correct explanation, the reader's model not stated and the yes or no to his question given first · scope: reports, explanations and questions an agent writes for a human reader, the operator of a coding session or a client, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code, at a part where the reader's registry line says his present answer does not hold · refuter: readers of the part in three steps give the correct account no more often, or score no higher, than readers of the same part with the explanation alone, by a test that tells the difference from chance · beside: external doi:10.1007/s10648-021-09656-z Results, Tables 2 and 3 first-hand; doi:10.1080/00461520.2024.2365628: a pre-registered meta-analysis of 76 studies, 111 samples and 294 effect sizes (total sample 10,265) of refutation texts against non-refutation texts on the same informational content gave more accurate beliefs after refutation, g = .37 (95% CI .26/.47, p < .001) over 222 post-only effects, and fewer inaccurate ones, g = -.38 (95% CI -.55/-.21, p < .01) over 14, moderators neither enhancing nor diminishing the effect, in the claim's direction under other conditions: students reading texts on science topics in articles of 1986 to 2021, the source not saying how many of its studies are among the 33 of doi:10.1007/s10648-021-09656-z; arXiv:2506.09292: in a pre-registered experiment 375 adults who rated a psychology misconception above 50 on a 0-100 scale and had explained in their own words why they held it were randomly assigned to a three-round Claude 3.5 Sonnet dialogue told to begin by acknowledging their initial perspective and then challenge that misconception, to a length-matched textbook-style passage that addressed it indirectly without explicitly mentioning or refuting it, or to a neutral dialogue, and belief in it right after was 9.77 points lower with the dialogue than with the passage (95% CI [3.02, 16.52], t(371) = 2.85, p = .013 by a Tukey contrast), 12.2 points lower at 10 days (95% CI [3.04, 21.30], p = .005) and 8.11 points lower at two months (95% CI [-17.3, 1.12], p ≈ .10), no longer reliable, in the claim's direction under other conditions: an interactive dialogue against a static passage, so that naming the participant's own belief is not separated from the dialogue, belief ratings rather than an account of a mechanism, no yes or no to a question, and a preprint that has not been peer reviewed; - **H-Explainer-the-first-part-gives-the-mechanism-whole-every-later-part-unfolds-one-named-line-of-it-a-thing-on-that-level-is-named-by-its-role** · an explanation whose first part after the opening gives the whole mechanism on one level — its signals with how each is measured and what each gives, the estimates made from them, the rule as pseudocode of a few steps, the states as a figure, a thing not yet unfolded named by its role with a link to its part — and whose every later part unfolds one named line of that level lets its readers retell the mechanism's steps from the page alone, and leaves fewer of their later questions already answered by the page, than the same matter ordered so that each thing is introduced before its use and the rule stands in a late part · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of the mechanism's steps a reader states correctly and in order after reading the page without its author and without the code, and the count of the readers' questions at their review whose answer the page already holds · against: the same matter in the same words ordered bottom-up: the routes, the table of questions and the table of terms after the opening, then the parts in the order that introduces each thing before its use, the rule in a late part · scope: explanations of a mechanism that an agent writes for several human readers, the team of the operator of a coding session, delivered as a page with voice, with 2026 Claude models in Claude Code · refuter: readers of the top-down page state no larger share of the steps correctly than readers of the bottom-up page, or ask as many questions whose answer the page already holds · beside: external doi:10.1037/1076-898X.8.3.147 abstract (Europe PMC record of PMID 12240927) abstract; - **H-Explaining-the-first-part-of-an-explanation-is-read-by-a-person-of-the-team-and-retold-cold-before-the-rest-is-written-a-retelling-marks-a-missing-step** · a reader with no context who is asked to retell a mechanism step by step from the first part of an explanation alone, each step tied to a sentence of the text, retells fewer steps than the author's rule holds when one step was removed from the text, its sentence and its line of pseudocode, more often than the same reader asked the questions of the registry about that part marks the removal · kind: claim · standing: hypothesis · measure: share of planted removals of one step from a first part on which the check of the cold reader's retelling fails, fewer steps standing than the author's rule holds or a step without a sentence of the text behind it, and the share on which an answer to a question is marked as not answered in full; the gaps a reader lists are counted apart and decide neither share · against: the cold read of the same text with the questions of the registry and no retelling · scope: first parts built for the measurement, each an opening and a mechanism of five steps with one step taken out by plan, read cold by a fresh agent on Claude Opus 5.5 at effort high in Claude Code; a first part its author wrote with a step left out, and a reader on another model, lie outside it · refuter: the check of the retelling fails on no larger share of the texts with a removed step than the answers to the questions mark, or it fails on the intact texts as often as on the texts with a removed step · threshold: the share of removals on which the check of the retelling fails is at least 40 points above the share the answers mark, by a one-sided Fisher exact test at p < 0.05, and the check fails on at most 3 of its 12 intact readings · runs: 12 readings of a text with a step removed and 12 of an intact text in each arm, four texts read three times · test: own plugins/evidence-based-authoring/skills/handing-over-to-readers/evals/grade_retelling.py · result: one run, `evals/retelling/runs/iteration-1`, went against the claim: the check of the retelling failed on 12 of 12 texts with a removed step and on 0 of 12 intact texts, and the answers to the questions marked 12 of 12 removals, each on the question about the steps answered in part, and 6 of 12 intact texts, each on the question about the example; the difference is 0 points against the threshold of 40, one-sided Fisher exact p = 1; in each of the 12 marking answers the reader names the number of steps the opening announces, so whether the answers mark a removal in a text that names no number is untested; a gap was listed in 12 of 12 retellings of a text with a removed step and in 2 of 12 of an intact one, counted apart; this is one failure, and the standing waits for its repetition · bounds: four first parts built for the measurement, each a mechanism of five steps whose opening names that number, with one step taken out, its sentence and its pseudocode line, while later sentences still use what the step produced; readers on Claude Opus 5.5 at effort high in Claude Code 2.1.331, under the cold reader's instructions of the plugin at version 10.2.0; 12 readings a cell · validity: direct: the claim is about the share of removals on which the check of the retelling fails, the quantity the grader counts · beside: external PMCID:PMC4144899 Results, Table 3, Overall row first-hand;
